Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergy tablet immunotherapy 1 year after a 3-year treatment period; Background: The sustained efficacy with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T, ALK-Abello, Denmark), 1 year after a 3-year treatment period, was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase III trial. Participants were adults with a history of moderate to severe grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis inadequately controlled by symptomatic medications. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 AIT, 126 placebo). All participants had free access to symptomatic medications during the grass pollen season. Primary and key secondary efficacy endpoints were rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by adverse events. Results: 1 year after treatment, the grass AIT group showed a 26% reduction in mean rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P = 0.022) when compared with placebo. The corresponding median reductions were 31% and 52%. This level of efficacy was similar to the efficacy during treatment. The mean difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P= 0.038) (median difference 49%) in favour of AIT. The grass AIT group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life (P= 0.004). No safety issues were identified. Conclusion: Significant and clinically relevant efficacy of grass AIT was sustained 1 year after a 3-year treatment period. This is the first time evidence of disease modification by sublingual tablet immunotherapy has been demonstrated in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial.

It was previously reported that the efficacy (symptom and medication scores) of the grass AIT was similar in monosensitized (grass only) and polysensitized (grass and ≥1 other allergen) patients. We investigated whether QoL improvement after 1 year of treatment with grass AIT was different for monosensitized vs. polysensitized patients. Patients were treated once-daily for approximately 16 weeks prior and throughout the grass pollen season. The SQ-standardized grass allergy immunotherapy tabletis well tolerated over 3 years of treatment; Introduction: Traditionally, allergen-specific immunotherapy has been administered via subcutaneous injections or sublingual drops. Recently, a new formulation administered sublingually, allergy immunotherapy tablets (AIT), has emerged. Many large double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trials have demonstrated AIT to be well tolerated and efficacious in reducing rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and the use of rescue medications. We investigated the nature, severity and duration of the most common adverse events (AEs) associated with treatment with an SQ-standardized grass AIT (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark) during a trial conducted over a 3 year period. Methods: Subjects with grass-allergy (n=634) were enrolled in a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational trial, of which 351 continued in a 2-year extension. Approval was obtained from the local IRBs and written informed consent obtained from all subjects. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to grass AIT or placebo. All AEs were collected and rated as mild, moderate or severe. AEs were assessed as treatment-emergent if the start date was on or after the date of the first tablet intake. Reactions occurring for a limited period of time each day on consecutive days were reported as one event. Results: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were oral pruritus (164 subjects, 191 events), mouth edema (65 subjects, 70 events), ear pruritus (41 subjects, 42 events) and throat irritation (34 subjects, 37 events). The majority of these AEs were mild (84%) or moderate (13%) and transient of nature. There was a sharp decrease in the percentage of subjects reporting these AEs within the first weeks ofthe trial (figure). Approximately 10% continued to have oral itching during the entire trial, typically lasting 10 minutes after tablet administration. Withdrawal due to these local AEs was low (n=6, 1%). Conclusions: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were local reactions affecting the mucosal membranes in mouth, ear and throat. The majority of AEs were mild and generally subsided shortly after treatment initiation, indicating development of tolerance. A small proportion of the subjects experienced local AEs daily upon tablet administration. However, the rate of withdrawals due to local AEs was low, indicating that grass AIT was well tolerated and these side effects were acceptable to most patients. (Figure Presented).
Rationale for a five-grass pollen vaccine; Grass pollen allergic patients are naturally exposed and sensitized to multiple pollens from various Pooideae species. The question arises as to whether such patients should be desensitized with extracts based on a single pollen or a pollen mixture. Neither conventional diagnosis based on IgE reactivity nor pollen counts enable the identification of which grass species are involved in patient sensitization. Significant cross-immunogenicity is observed between allergens from Pooideae pollens due to their conserved amino acid sequences (e.g. >90% for group 1, 55-80% for group 5, 30-60% for other allergens, including minor allergens). Nevertheless, pollen allergens also contain species-specific T or B cell epitopes, and there is evidence that at least 50% of allergic patients are sensitized to such distinct epitopes. In addition, substantial quantitative differences exist in the allergen (e.g. group 1, 2/3, 4, 5, 6, 10, 13) content of pollens obtained from distinct grass species. In this context, we recommend a vaccine consisting of a combination of pollens from five common and well characterized Pooideae including Anthoxanthum odoratum, Dactylis glomerata, Lolium perenne, Phleum pratense and Poa pratensis for desensitization purposes. This 5-grass mixture is recommended because, (i) it has been validated, in terms of safety and efficacy, by established clinical practice
Efficacy of grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy for three consecutive seasons and after cessation of treatment: The ECRIT study; Background: Data supporting a carry-over effect with sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) are scarce. This randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study evaluated the efficacy, carry-over effect and safety of grass pollen SLIT using co-seasonal treatment. Methods: Patients (7.9-64.7 years) with grass pollen allergy received ultra-rush titration with increasing doses (30, 90, 150 and 300 IR) of a 5-grass pollen mixture every 20 min at the start of the pollen seasons, followed by 300 IR daily until the end of the pollen seasons. A baseline season (no SLIT) was followed by three consecutive treatment seasons and one follow-up season. Symptoms, medication and adverse events were documented and specific immunoglobulin (Ig)E and IgG4 measured. Results: Data were analysed for 183 of the 213 randomized patients. Mean treatment duration varied between seasons (81.8-92.7 days). Combined scores (symptoms and medication) improved progressively across treatment seasons (up to 44.7% improvement for SLIT compared with baseline) and fluctuated between -11.3% and -14.8% for placebo (P < 0.05). Similar changes were observed for symptom scores, with a successive decrease of 39.7% (SLIT) and fluctuations between +13.6% and -1.51% for placebo (P < 0.05). Combined score (P = 0.0508) and symptom score improvements (P = 0.0144) with SLIT continued during follow up. Increases in specific IgG4 observed in the first season were sustained for SLIT vs placebo throughout treatment (P = 0.0001). Titration and daily SLIT were well tolerated. No serious systemic or anaphylactic reactions were reported. Conclusions: Seasonal SLIT with ultra-rush titration is well tolerated and effective from the first treatment season onwards. These data indicate a carry-over effect of seasonal SLIT. © 2008 The Authors.
Early onset of action of a 5-grass-pollen 300-IR sublingual immunotherapy tablet evaluated in an allergen challenge chamber; Background: The efficacy and safety of a 5-grass-pollen sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet (Stallergènes SA, Antony, France) have been evaluated in clinical studies during the pollen season. The allergen challenge chamber (ACC) has been developed as a pharmacodynamic assessment tool to control the environmental allergens and to avoid all problems associated with unpredictable pollen seasons. Objective: We sought to evaluate the onset of action and efficacy of 300-IR (index of reactivity) SLIT tablets by using an ACC. Methods: Patients with grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized into the active or placebo groups. A standardized allergen challenge with grass pollen and symptom evaluation every 15 minutes was performed at baseline, 1 week, and 1, 2, and 4 months of treatment. Response to sublingual immunotherapy with grass pollen extract: Monotherapy versus combination in a multiallergen extract; Background: To date, there have been no randomized, double-blind studies showing the effectiveness of sublingual immunotherapy with multiple allergens. Objective: The purpose of this study was to examine whether the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) with standardized timothy extract was reduced by combination with other allergen extracts. Methods: A single-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial with SLIT was conducted. After an observational grass season, SLIT was administered for 10 months to 54 patients randomized to 1 of 3 treatment arms: placebo, timothy extract (19 μg Phl p 5 daily) as monotherapy, or the same dose of timothy extract plus 9 additional pollen extracts. Symptom and medication scores were collected and titrated nasal challenges, titrated skin prick tests, specific IgE, IgG4 and cytokines release by timothy-stimulated lymphocyte proliferation were performed. Results: Perhaps because of a very low grass pollen season in 2008, there were no significant differences in medication or symptom scores in either treatment group compared with placebo. Compared with placebo, in the timothy monotherapy group, thresholds for titrated nasal challenge and skin prick tests (P = .03 and P = .001, respectively), and serum-specific IgG4 levels (P = .005) significantly increased, and IFN- γ levels decreased (P = .02), whereas in the multiallergen group, there was significant improvement only in the titrated skin prick tests (P = .04) which was less than in the monotherapy group. There were no significant differences between the 2 active groups in any outcome measure, and both active groups experienced more adverse events than placebo. There were no systemic reactions. Conclusion: Improvement in multiple relevant outcomes strongly suggests that SLIT with timothy extract alone was effective
Administration regimens for sublingual immunotherapy to pollen allergens: What do we know?; The modalities of administration of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT), including dosing, build-up phase, duration of the treatment, and frequency of the maintenance dose are largely variable. In the case of pollen (SLIT), the complexity increases, since preseasonal, coseasonal and pre-coseasonal regimens can be used. The administration regimens are of relevance from a practical point of view, but can also have economic implications. We review herein the available literature (randomized double blind controlled studies) on pollen SLIT, in order to derive experimentally-supported suggestions about the regimens of administration that should be preferred. © 2009 John Wiley & Sons A/S.
Foxp3 is increased in the sublingual mucosa after grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells; Background: Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) using an SQ-standardised extract of Phleum Pratense (ALK Abello, Horsholm Denmark, daily drops containing 25 μg Phleum p5 daily for 12-18 months) resulted in improvements in patients' overall assessment of hayfever severity, suppression of the late skin response and an increase in serum IgG4 level, but no change in T cell numbers, CD1a+ Langerhans' cells or IL-12 mRNA expression in the sublingual mucosa (Lima M, Clin Exp Allergy 2002). We now report measurements of dendritic cell subsets, mast cells, T cells and Foxp3 as a marker of T regulatory cells in the sublingual mucosa and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells (IgE-FAB). Methods: Cryostat/paraformaldhyde sections from the sublingual mucosa from 14 SLIT-treated, nine placebo treated and eight normal controls were immunostained for myeloid dendritic cells (CD1c), plasmacytoid dendritic cells (CD303), mast cells (AA1) and T cells (CD3) using dual-immunofluorescence microscopy. Sections from approximately six subjects from each group were also immunostained for Foxp3 and IL-9. Positive cells were counted blind and expressed per mm2 in the epithelium and subepithelium. Serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB was measured at different time points. Results: Epithelial myeloid dendritic cell (mDC) numbers were similar in each group. There were greater subepithelial mDCs in placebo treated patients than normal controls (P = 0.05). Few plasmacytoid dendritic (pDC) cells were seen in any group and were largely confined to the subepithelium. There were possibly fewer subepithelial mast cells and more epithelial T cells in SLIT-compared to placebo-treated subjects (P = 0.1 for both). Foxp3+ cells within the epithelium were present in five of seven SLIT-treated patients compared to zero of six placebo-treated patients (P = 0.014, Chi-Square test) and Foxp3 co-localised to T cells in two subjects in whom dual immunofluorescence was informative. There was a time-dependent increase in serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB (P < 0.001), maximal at 6 weeks when compared to placebo-treated patients. Conclusion: These data suggest that SLIT may operate through similar mechanisms to SCIT and is associated with recruitment of T cells including possible Foxp3+ Tregs to the sublingual epithelium, a possible reduction in subepithelial mast cells and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and IgG-associated inhibition of IgE-facilitated allergen presentation.

Recent data have shown that continuous SLIT therapy (i.e. 12 consecutive months) offers a greater clinical benefit in comparison with shorter SLIT treatment courses. Therefore compliance to the SLIT is an important aspect for a positive clinical outcome. Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergen tablet immunotherapy 1 year after completion of treatment; Background: The sustained efficacy following withdrawal of ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello, Hørsholm, Denmark) was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase III trial. Participants were followed for 1 year after completion of a 3-year period of daily treatment. Participants were adults with a clinical history of moderate to severe grass pollen allergy inadequately controlled by the use of symptomatic medication. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 active, 126 placebo) who entered the follow-up investigation 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Efficacy endpoints comprised rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included serum levels of grass pollen specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by means of adverse events. Results: One year after completion of treatment the active group showed a 26% reduction in rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P < 0.02) when compared with placebo over the entire grass pollen season. This level of sustained efficacy between active and placebo groups was not significantly different compared to the previous season, i.e. during treatment. The difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P = 0.04) in favour of AIT. Participants in the active group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life both during the entire grass pollen season and during the peak season. Sustained clinical benefit after treatment completion was accompanied by persistent immunological changes as observed for subcutaneous immunotherapy. Six mild adverse events were assessed as treatmentrelated. Eight serious events (four in each group) were all assessed as unlikely related to treatment. Conclusion: This double-blind analysis 1 year after completion of a 3-year period of daily all year round treatment showed significant effects on the primary and key secondary endpoints, thereby demonstrating sustained efficacy with ALK grass AIT following completion of treatment. This is the first time that evidence of disease modification by allergen tablet immunotherapy has been shown in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial. No safety issues were identified.
Efficacy and safety of a 300IR five-grass pollen SLIT tablet in children and adolescents with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: The efficacy and safety of a five-grass pollen 300IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet (Stallergenes SA, France) has recently been confirmed in children and adolescents with grass pollen allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Secondary and complementary analyses on the trial's intention-to-treat (ITT) population were performed for the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS), the six Average Individual Symptom Scores (AISSs) and for safety parameters according to age class (5 to 11 and 12 to 17). Methods: Two hundred and seventy-eight children (5-17 years of age) with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis were included in a multinational, randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled phase III trial. They were treated with once-daily five grasspollen SLIT tablets or placebo tablets 4 months prior to and then throughout the pollen season. Each symptom was evaluated daily by the patients (with parental assistance, for the youngest) on a four-point scale from 0 (absent) to 3 (severe). Results: The difference between 300IR and placebo tablets was significant for rhinorrhea (P = 0.038), nasal congestion (P = 0.0012), ocular pruritus (P < 0.0001) and watery eyes (P = 0.0008). Improvements in sneezing and nasal pruritus were not statistically significant. Patients aged 12 to 17 had higher ARTSSs and AISSs than those aged 5 to 11 years whatever the treatment group and the analysed period (pollen season or pollen peak). In both age classes (12-17 and 5-11), the ARTSS was lower in the 300IR group (3.77 and 2.94, respectively) than in the placebo group (4.98 and 4.08, respectively). A complementary subgroup analysis on safety parameters revealed a similar safety profile of the 300IR five-grass pollen sublingual tablet in age subgroups 5-11 and 12-17, as well as in sub-goups 5-7 and 8-11. Conclusion: Five-grass pollen tablet immunotherapy has a significant effect on eye and nasal symptoms such as nasal congestion and rhinorrhea and was in this study an effective treatment for grass polleninduced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in children and adolescents, independently of age class. Also, the same dose can be administered to allergic children and adolescents aged 5-17 years without any influence on the tolerability of the treatment.
Early onset of action of a five-grass pollen 300 IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet evaluated in an allergen Challenge Chamber; Background: The efficacy and safety of the 300 IR grass pollen SLIT tablet have been evaluated in clinical studies performed during the pollen season. However, no information on the onset of action of any type of SLIT has been reported. The Vienna Challenge Chamber (VCC) was developed as a pharmacodynamic assessment tool in which highly reproducible allergen challenge sessions are used to overcome problems related to pollen season unpredictability. Methods: Patients with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized into either active (300 IR SLIT tablets, od) (Oralair® Grasses Stallergenes, France) or placebo groups treated for 4 months out of the season, without rescue medication usage. A standardized grass pollen challenge (at a stable count of 1450-1500 grains per m, with symptom evaluation every 15 min) was performed at baseline and after 1 week and 1, 2 and 4 months of treatment. The primary efficacy endpoint was the active versus placebo difference in the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS) after 4 months or at the last challenge. Allergen-specific basophil activation, T cell proliferation and plasma IgE and IgG responses were also assessed before and after 4 months of treatment. Results: In the ITT population (n = 89), a significant treatment effect was seen after the 1st month (P = 0.0042) and 2nd month (P = 0.02) of SLIT and was maintained through to month 4 (P = 0.0007). In the active group, the ARTSS decreased at each challenge, i.e. week 1 (7.40 ± 2.68), month 1 (5.89 ± 2.43), month 2 (5.09 ± 2.09) and month 4 (4.85 ± 2.0). The SLIT was well tolerated. The most frequent adverse events (>5% in at least one group) were local and as expected: oral pruritus, throat irritation and ear pruritus. Of the immunological parameters tested, only induction of allergen-specific IgGs was associated with a clinical response. Conclusion: For the first time ever, the onset of action and efficacy of Oralair®Grasses SLIT tablets have been assessed (versus placebo) in an ACC. The effect of this 300 IR grass pollen SLIT tablet on rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms was statistically significant (versus placebo) from the first month of treatment onward.
The improvement in disease specific quality of life is sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment with the a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet; Background: Grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis affects 15-25% of the European population. Seventy percent of all allergic patients report that their condition significantly impairs their quality of life (QoL). Three years of all year round treatment with the ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello), has previously been shown to reduce rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and medication use. Importantly, this was accompanied by a significant improvement in the QoL of the patients. In this trial we investigated whether the improvement in QoL was sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial in which patients were randomised to receive 3 years of either active treatment (n = 157) or placebo (n = 126), followed by 1 year of follow-up to investigate the long-term effects after treatment withdrawal. Disease specific QoL was assessed after the 1st year of follow-up based on Juniper's Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire (RQLQ), which was completed by the patients weekly during the entire grass pollen season. The RQLQ defines QoL based on seven individual domains (activities, sleep, non-nose/eye symptoms, practical problems, nasal problems, eye problems, and emotional) assessed on a scale from 0-6 (0: 'not troubled', 6: 'extremely troubled'). Results: The overall RQLQ score (mean of the seven domains) for the entire season (average length = 65 days) was significantly improved in patients receiving the ALK grass AIT compared with placebo (23%, P = 0.004, see table). This improvement was even more pronounced during the peak grass pollen season (length = 15 days, difference = 28%, P = 0.001). All seven domains were improved in patients receiving active treatment (data not shown). Conclusion: These results demonstrate that the previously observed improvement of disease specific QoL in adult patients suffering from grass allergy receiving 3 years of daily all year round treatment with the ALK grass AIT is sustained 1 year following completion of treatment indicating a disease modifying effect of therapy.

Grass allergen tablet immunotherapy in children induces IgG4 antibody and 4IgE-blocking responses similar to those seen in adults; Background: Serum antibody responses were investigated after ALK grass allergen tablet immunotherapy (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2800 BAU, ALK-Abelló) in children and results compared to those obtained after the same treatment in adults. Methods: In randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in children (5-16 year) and adults (18-65 year), AIT was initiated prior to the grass pollen season, and once-daily treatment was continued until the end of the season. All participants had a clinical history of moderate to severe grass allergy. The levels of Phleum pratense specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor (i.e. serum components competing with IgE for allergen binding) were assessed in serum samples before, during and after the grass pollen season. Results: In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (P < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months of treatment was 32 U/l. In adults, 8 months of treatment led to an increase in the active group of 45.7 U/l from the initial level (P < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor increased in the active AIT groups in both children and adults (significantly different treatment effect in the active groups compared to the placebo groups, P < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference from the initial level in IgE-blocking factor in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the difference was 0.15 after 8 months. The adult placebo group showed no significant change while the paediatric placebo group showed a slight increase in IgE-blocking factor during the grass pollen season. The clinical treatment effect in children by means of the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score and the percentage of well days was also similar to that observed in adults when looking at comparable pollen exposure periods. Conclusion: These data confirm comparable elevations in serum IgG4 antibodies and IgE-blocking activity in children compared with adults following successful treatment with ALK grass AIT. In addition, the higher seasonal elevations in IgE-blocking activity in placebo-treated children compared with adults, suggest that children may establish a more tolerogenic rather than allergic immune response during the grass pollen season. This observation suggests that the use of AIT in childhood may ameliorate the ongoing natural course of the disease more efficiently than AIT during adulthood.
The effect of the matched pairs method on the measurement of treatment effect in a trial of ultra short course specific immunotherapy; Background: A common problem during the assessment of specific immunotherapy (SIT) in patients with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis is incomplete data from patient diaries during the pollen season. Such data gaps may be filled using the matched pairs method, but this process has the potential to distort the data and invite misleading conclusions. Here we compare whole-population, matched-pair data from a large Phase III trial with the subgroup of patients for whom complete data were available to assess the effect of the matched pairs method. Methods: In a large, randomised, Phase III trial, 1028 patients with moderate-to-severe allergic rhinoconjunctivitis to grass pollen received ultra short course specific immunotherapy (uSCIT) or placebo. Efficacy was measured using a combined symptom and medication score (CSMS) from patient diary data collected during the four peak weeks of pollen exposure. We compared data for the whole population, which utilised a matched pairs method to fill data gaps, with data from the subset of subjects for whom complete data were available. Results: There were 343 subjects for whom 28 diary entries were available during the four peak weeks of the pollen season (177 uSCIT, 166 placebo). In both the whole population and the complete data subgroup, uSCIT was found to be significantly more effective than placebo, with improvements in the CSMS of 13.4% (P = 0.0038) and 26.9% (P = 0.0031) for the whole population and complete data groups, respectively. There was a similar pattern for symptom scores alone (eyes and nose), with improvements for uSCIT compared with placebo of 10.9% (P = 0.0132) and 24.7% (P = 0.0181) for the whole population and complete data groups, respectively. Conclusion: The disparity in the primary endpoint between the whole population and the subgroup with complete data was considerable. Although a significant benefit for uSCIT compared with placebo was detected in each set of data, the size of the effect was approximately doubled when complete data were used to compare the treatment groups. It is therefore apparent that using the matched pairs method to compensate for incomplete data may not improve the precision of the estimate of treatment effect for the whole population, in this case yielding a 50% underestimate of treatment efficacy in approximately one third of subjects. This phenomenon represents a significant challenge in the assessment of SIT formulations for allergic rhinoconjunctivitis.

Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy with a high-dose 6-grass pollen preparation progressively increases in the third treatment year; Background: Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) depends on a sufficiently high maintenance dose as recently confirmed by the ARIA workshop report (2008 update). Often SLIT trials last for at most 2 years, and only few data are available about the effect of further treatment years. Progressive efficacy of a high-dose Single Dose grass pollen preparation has been shown in a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial for the first two treatment years. The preparation was standardized to the content of 40 μg grasses group 5 allergens in the maintenance dose. Aim of this study was to investigate the effect of a third treatment year. Methods: After a baseline period in 2003 grass pollen-allergic patients (rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma) received treatment with AllerSlit forte or placebo for two consecutive years (2004 and 2005). Data from 39 of the former 49 actively treated patients in the Main Data Set (MDS) were available in the third, open treatment year (2006). Symptom and medication scores, and the number of 'well days' (days with a symptom score ≤4 and without use of any rescue medication) were evaluated. Results: Symptom and medication scores were further improved during the third treatment year in comparison to the year before. The percentage of patients who did not need any rescue medication during the 42-day observation period in the grass pollen season continuously increased during the study: 0% at baseline, 19% at the end of the double-blind phase in 2005 to 31% in the third treatment year. Additionally, the median number of 'well days' increased during the 3 years: from 6/42 days at baseline pollen season, and 19.5/42 days after two treatment years in 2005 to 35/42 'well days' after 3 years of SLIT. The high-dose preparation was well tolerated with mainly mild adverse reactions. No serious drugrelated adverse events occurred. Conclusion: The third year of SLIT with the high-dose grass pollen preparation caused an additional benefit for the patients. Allergic symptoms and the intake of rescue medication were further reduced during the grass pollen season. The high number of 'well days' affirms the progressive efficacy.

A multicentre study to evaluate the adherence of sublingual and Depot immunotherapy to mixed grass in patients with rhinoconjunctivitis and/or asthma: The polite study; Background: Compliance is a major determinant for allergy treatment. The aim of this post-marketing surveillance study was to compare the adherence of sublingual (Sublivac, Haarlem Allergen Labs BV, Haarlem, The Netherlands) and Depot (Purethal) immunotherapy to mixed grass. Methods: The adherence of the treatment was assessed by a case report form designed specifically for the study. Compliance was considered as appropriate when the treatment was adequately administered during the study period. Data were collected over for 7 months. Sixty patients (27 female) from 8 to 58 years (30.5 ± 12.3 years) were included. All individuals have been diagnosed of rhinoconjunctivitis and/or asthma before the start of the study. The treatment was administered pre-seasonally according to the usual dosage schedule of both treatments. Results: Thirty-four patients had rhinoconjunctivitis + asthma, 22 rhinoconjunctivitis, 2 rhinitis, and 2 rhinitis + asthma. Purethal and Sublivac were prescribed in 21 (35%) and 39 (65%) subjects, respectively. At the end of the study, 79% of subjects had an appropriate compliance of the treatment. The treatment was discontinued with Sublivac and Purethal in 8% and 13% cases, respectively. The treatment was discontinued in Sublivac because of wrong treatment fulfilment and in Purethal for not requesting the maintenance treatment. Conclusions: The results presented here indicate that the short-term compliance for allergy treatment is good and it's not influenced by the administration route. The data obtained support that the compliance with Sublivac is adequate despite the therapy given at home.
Relation between grass pollen exposure and efficacy for a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet; Background: Pollen levels display extreme day-to-day variation. Hence, post hoc analyses exploring the effect of grass pollen exposure on efficacy measures and observed differences between treatment groups were performed for a phase III trial investigating the efficacy and safety in children of the once-daily ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU). Methods: A total of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1 : 1 to receive either active treatment or placebo prior to and throughout the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups in a step-wise fashion. Statistical analyses of efficacy parameters were performed with two different definitions of the pollen season, using 10 or 30 grains/m3 as threshold. In addition, the effect of pollen exposure was explored by investigating the efficacy for days with low, medium and high pollen counts and by modelling the dependence of the efficacy scores on the pollen count for each treatment group. Results: Statistically significant differences between active and placebo demonstrated effect of the ALK grass AIT throughout the entire grass pollen season for both pollen season definitions. The definition of the pollen season influenced the efficacy outcome. Using 10 grains/m3, the relative differences between active and placebo were 22% for both the mean symptom score and for well days. These differences increased upon replacing the threshold with 30 grains/m3, to 28% for the symptom score and 44% for well days. On days with high pollen counts (>50 grains/m3), symptoms increased compared to days with low pollen counts (≤10 grains/m3), from a daily average of 2.1 to 3.8 for the placebo group and from 1.7 to 2.7 for the active group. Simultaneously the average medication score increased from 0.8 to 2.4 for the placebo group but only from 0.4 to 0.9 for the active group. Modelling of the efficacy scores showed a substantial increase in the difference between active and placebo upon increasing pollen load. Conclusion: This was the first phase III trial investigating the ALK grass AIT in children. A clear statistically significant difference between active and placebo was seen throughout the entire grass pollen season, in favour of active treatment. On days with high pollen counts the clinical benefit of the ALK grass AIT was markedly increased, both for symptoms and for the use of symptomatic medication.
Treatment with a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet in children above and below the age of 10 - efficacy, safety and immunological response; Background: Treatment with the ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU), is efficacious and well-tolerated in adult grass-allergic patients. Younger children are often perceived as good candidates for immunotherapy, and post hoc analyses to explore possible differences between younger and older children were performed for an ALK grass AIT paediatric phase III trial. Methods: A total of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1 : 1 to receive either active treatment or placebo prior to and throughout the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups. The population was divided into two age groups comprising 140 children aged 5-10 years and 113 children aged 11-16 years. The effect of age on efficacy was also analysed by including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis. Results: Including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis indicated age to have no influence on the efficacy, as demonstrated by a P-value of 0.6 for the interaction between age and treatment. Analysis of the difference between active and placebo for each age group showed a slightly higher relative difference in symptom score for the younger children compared with the older, 26% versus 19%. The difference between active and placebo was statistically significant for the 5-10 year old (P = 0.047) although the trial was powered for analysis of the population as a whole. In the active group the percentage of adverse events (AEs) assessed as treatment-related was nominally lower for the younger group than for the older children, 32% versus 40%. The percentages of AEs assessed as moderate or severe in the active group were similar for the two age groups. For both active and placebo, the overall number of AEs was nominally higher in the younger group than in the older group. The immunological response (IgE, IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor) was similar for the two age groups. There was a tendency for the younger group towards an increased response in IgE-blocking factor with onset of the grass pollen season, evident in both the actively treated and the placebo group. Conclusion: This was the first phase III trial investigating the ALK grass AIT in children. Once-daily administration was found to be clinically efficacious and welltolerated. The effect was comparable for children below and above the age of 10, as was the safety pattern and the immunological response.

We have now explored effect of the grass AIT on all individual nose and eye symptoms 1 year after completion of a 3 year course of treatment. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial in which patients were randomised to receive 3 years of either active treatment (n = 157) or placebo (n = 126), followed by 1 year of follow-up to investigate the long-term effects after grass AIT treatment. The rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score was based on 6 rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms (runny nose, blocked nose, sneezing, itchy nose, itchy eyes and watery eyes), that were scored on a daily basis on a scale with the following values: 0 (no symptoms)
In-season initiation of treatment with SQ standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet induces an immunomodulatory response and seems to be well tolerated; Background: Clinical efficacy and a favourable safety profile of the SQ standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT, Grazax®, Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T, ALK-Abelló) have been demonstrated in several trials. The current recommendation is to start treatment 4 months prior to the grass pollen season and to continue treatment for 3 consecutive years. A large proportion of allergy patients have their first visit to the physician during the pollen season, i.e. when they experience their symptoms. Currently, these subjects are offered symptomatic therapy and the opportunity to start immunotherapy after the pollen season. For these patients, it would be of interest to be able to start treatment in the season. Therefore, in the present trial the pharmacodynamic effect and tolerability of in-season initiation of AIT treatment were evaluated. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial conducted in 28 centres in Germany and Austria. A total of 276 subjects with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma were randomised 4 : 1 to receive either active (N = 219) or placebo (N = 57) once daily, starting during the grass pollen season and continuing at least 8 weeks (+ 1-2 weeks). Pharmacodynamic endpoints included the change from baseline of IgE-blocking factor, IgE, and IgG4. Tolerability assessment was based on adverse events. Results: An immunomodulatory effect of in-season treatment was demonstrated with statistically significant higher inductions of IgE-blocking factor (P < 0.0001), IgE (P < 0.0001) and IgG4 (P <.0001) in the active group as compared with placebo. Overall, in-season treatment was well tolerated and comparable to what has been observed in other clinical trials in which treatment was initiated outside the pollen season. Most of the reported adverse events were mild or moderate in severity. The majority of events related to treatment were local reactions, mainly oral pruritus. Four serious adverse events, all considered unlikely related to treatment, were reported during the trial. No safety concerns were observed regarding FEV1, vital sign or physical examinations. Conclusion: In-season initiation of treatment with the grass AIT induces an immunomodulatory response in subjects with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis. Furthermore, in-season initiation was found to be well tolerated, however a suitable powered trial would be required to confirm the safety profile.
With increased grass pollen exposure, treatment with SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet further improves quality of life compared to symptomatic medications alone; Background: We explored the relation between rhinoconjunctivitis disease specific quality of life, treatment with grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT, Grazax, Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abelló) and grass pollen exposure. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial in which patients were randomised to receive 3 years of either active or placebo, followed by 1 year of follow-up. Disease specific QoL was assessed weekly during the entire grass pollen season by Juniper's Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire (RQLQ). The RQLQ defines QoL based on 7 individual domains assessed on a scale from 0 to 6 (0: “not troubled”, 6: “extremely troubled”). The relationship between RQLQ scores and pollen load is described with a local regression model. Results: The predicted RQLQ as a function of the weekly average pollen counts show a clear separation between the two treatment groups (p < 0.001) in favour of the grass AIT, and the difference increased with increased pollen counts during the entire grass pollen season. RQLQ scores during the entire and peak grass pollen seasons are shown in the table below: Conclusion: SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet provides improved disease specific quality of life compared to symptomatic medications alone, with increased effect as grass pollen exposure increase. Quality of life was improves during all 3 years of treatment and was sustained 1 year after treatment.
An economic evaluation of grazax for the treatment of grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis in children; OBJECTIVES: To determine the cost-effectiveness of the grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (ALK-Abelló A/S) for the treatment of grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis in children either with or without co-existing asthma. The analysis compares treatment using Grazax in combination with symptomatic medications, to using symptomatic medications alone in terms of costs and health outcomes. METHODS: The model uses a decision tree structure to evaluate two grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis treatments for a cohort of 1000 hypothetical children. The analysis is performed from the perspective of the payer, including only direct costs in the base case. Treatment is modelled in terms of the management of symptoms, reduction in resource use and averting the development of allergic asthma. The evaluation is UK-based and models both short- and long-term effects. Several data inputs, such as resource use and probabilities, are drawn from the paediatric GT-12 clinical trial and the Preventive Allergy Treatment (PAT) study. Cost data are sourced from established published sources and utility values are derived using GT-12 trial data and a published study. RESULTS: Treatment with Grazax in combination with symptomatic medications compared to treatment with symptomatic medications alone generates a (discounted) incremental cost per QALY gained of 10,007GBP ($14,578, ε11,034), for the base case time horizon of nine years. The QALY gains are a result of increased quality of life related to effective symptom management. The sensitivity analysis carried out around key parameters (e.g. unit costs, medication costs, probabilities, utilities) shows that the results estimated by the model remain robust. CONCLUSIONS: Grazax has been shown to improve patient outcomes, at an increased cost. The resulting incremental cost per QALY falls below commonly accepted willingness to pay thresholds. Therefore, Grazax is a cost-effective option for the treatment of grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis in the UK paediatric population.

Implementation of pre-seasonal sublingual immunotherapy with a five-grass pollen tablet during optimal dosage assessment; Background: The optimal dose (300IR) of a five-grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablet in terms of efficacy was previously demonstrated from the first pollen season. Objective: Here, we aim to confirm whether this dose remained optimal during the peak of the pollen season by assessing the efficacy and quality of life data. Methods: A total of 628 subjects with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized in a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multi-centre, pan-European trial. Subjects received once-daily tablets (Stallergenes, Antony, France) of 100IR, 300IR, 500IR or placebo, starting 4 months before and throughout the 2005 grass pollen season. The pollen season was defined as the first day of 3 consecutive days with a grass pollen count above 30 grains/m3 of air, recorded using Hirst-type volumetric pollen traps, to the last day before 3 consecutive days with a pollen count below 30 grains/m3. Results: The grass pollen season lasted an average of 30 days, with a peak of 12 days. The mean treatment duration before the grass pollen season was similar in the four treatment groups (121.4±31.1 to 128.6±15.4 days in the safety population). Both the 300IR and 500IR groups had highly significant improvements in Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (RTSS) vs. placebo at the peak pollen season (P=0.0005 and 0.0014, respectively), which agreed with improvements in RTSS in the primary evaluations. The average RTSS scores were slightly elevated during the peak pollen season in all treatment groups. The overall Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire score confirmed the optimal dosage 300IR at peak (P<0.0001) and at the end (P≤0.0031) of the pollen season. All doses were well tolerated. Conclusion: At the peak pollen season, the efficacy and quality of life data for both 300IR and 500IR groups was significantly improved vs. the placebo group. These results confirm the conclusions of the primary evaluations and validate the use of 300IR tablets for clinical practice. © 2008 Stallergenes.

Evaluation of a five grass-pollen Sublingual Immunotherapy Tablets (SLIT) in a pediatric population at peak pollen season and immunological outcomes; RATIONALE: The efficacy and safety of grass-pollen SLIT tablets in children (aged 5 to 17) have been established using the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS) as a primary endpoint, in a single-season, double-blind, placebo controlled Phase III study. Secondary and exploratory analyses, such as the ARTSS improvement at the pollen peak and levels of immunological markers are presented. METHODS: 278 children, with grass-pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized to receive daily either a 300 IR SLIT tablets or placebo starting 4 months before the pollen season and throughout the latter. The peak pollen period was defined as the 10-14 consecutive days with the highest mean pollen counts. Immunological markers and skin prick tests (SPTs) were evaluated before and after treatment RESULTS: Between groups, there was a highly significant (p = 0.0009) ARTSS difference. At the pollen peak, the improvement versus placebo was 28% (mean) and 43% (median). The geometric mean IgG4 level (μg/ L) more than tripled from baseline to endpoint for children receiving 300IR (ratio 3.37) but did not change in the placebo group (ratio 1.41). In contrast, the geometric mean IgE level (kU/L) was similar at baseline and endpoint for 300IR (ratio 1.35), but slightly higher post-treatment for placebo (ratio1.64). Concerning the SPTs, patients on 300IR had a larger mean decrease in wheal diameter (-1.73 mm) than those on placebo (-0.77 mm). CONCLUSIONS: As for the entire pollen season, the efficacy of 300 IR SLIT grass pollen tablets was confirmed for the pollen peak. In children receiving SLIT, specific IgG4 levels clearly increased while specific IgE levels remained stable.

Significant reduction in combined symptom and medication score compared with placebo following MPL-adjuvanted uSCIT in patients with seasonal grass pollen allergy; RATIONALE: The efficacy and tolerability of ultra short course subcutaneous immunotherapy (uSCIT) adjuvanted with monophosphoryl lipid A (MPL), a Toll-like Receptor (TLR) 4 agonist, in the treatment of seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis (SAR) to grass pollen was assessed. METHODS: This multicenter, double-blind, placebo-controlled study included 1028 grass pollen-allergic subjects. Subjects received a pre-seasonal, 4-injection course of uSCIT containing a 13 grass pollen allergoid (300, 800, 2000, and 2000 SU) plus 50 mg MPL per injection in an L-tyrosine depot (Pollinex Quattro®) at approximately weekly intervals. Efficacy was assessed by combined symptom and medication score (CSMS) using patient diaries completed throughout the grass pollen season. Local and systemic reactions were recorded during the 24 hours following each injection. RESULTS: There was a 13.4% improvement (p = 0.0038) in CSMS in patients who received uSCIT (n = 514) compared with placebo (n = 513) over the four peak pollen weeks. In those subjects who had no missing data during the peak pollen period, the improvement in CSMS was 26.9% for uSCIT compared with placebo (p = 0.0031). Adverse events (AEs) were predominantly local and discontinuations due to AEs were infrequent (1.4% for uSCIT, 0.8% for placebo), with no treatment-related anaphylaxis. Following each injection, >60% of treated patients experienced no AEs. CONCLUSIONS: Pre-seasonal uSCIT was highly effective and well tolerated, with a clinically significant reduction in SAR symptoms. Adverse events were predominantly local and mild to moderate in nature, indicating a favourable tolerability profile. This is the first large Phase III study of specific allergen immunotherapy for SAR to meet its primary efficacy endpoint.
Agreement of efficacy assessments for five-grass pollen sublingual tablet immunotherapy; Background: The optimal dose of five-grass pollen sublingual tablet immunotherapy (SLIT) was established recently by the primary criteria Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (RTSS) from the first treatment season. Secondary and exploratory criteria, such as RTSS at peak pollen season, exploratory combined symptom and rescue medication use score, quality of life and immunological markers are calculated and described in this analysis. Methods: Six hundred and twenty-eight patients with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis (≥2 years duration) were randomized in a double-blind, placebo-controlled trial conducted in Europe. Specific immunoglobulin G4 increased significantly depending on the SLIT dose (P < 0.0001). Safety and efficacy in children of an SQ-standardized grass allergen tablet for sublingual immunotherapy; Background: Immunotherapy with the SQ-standardized grass tablet Grazax is efficacious and well-tolerated in adult patients with rhinoconjunctivitis. Allergic asthma and rhinoconjunctivitis are closely linked, and a strategy combining treatment of the upper and lower airways is recommended. Objective: To investigate the efficacy of treatment with the grass tablet on grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis and asthma as well as the immunologic response and the safety profile in children. Methods: A total of 253 children age 5 to 16 years, with grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis with/without asthma, were randomized 1:1 to active treatment or placebo. Treatment was initiated 8 to 23 weeks before the start of the grass pollen season 2007 and continued throughout the entire season. Symptomatic medication was provided as relief medication to both groups in a stepwise fashion. Primary endpoints were rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores. Results: The rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores and the asthma symptom score were all statistically significantly different between the 2 treatment groups. The differences in medians relative to placebo were 24%, 34%, and 64% in favor of active treatment. The immunologic response was similar to that observed in adults. The most common adverse reaction was oral pruritus, reported by 40 subjects (32%) in the active and 3 (2%) in the placebo group. Six subjects withdrew because of adverse events. No serious adverse events were assessed as treatment-related. Conclusion: Immunotherapy with the grass tablet reduced grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis and asthma symptoms in a pediatric population and introduced an immunomodulatory response, consistent with treatment of the underlying allergic disease. The treatment was well tolerated. © 2009 American Academy of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology.
Efficacy and safety of 5-grass-pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablets in pediatric allergic rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: The efficacy and safety of the 300-index of reactivity (IR) dose of 5-grass-pollen sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets (Stallergènes, Antony, France) have been demonstrated for the treatment of hay fever in adults. Methods: In this multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study, 278 children (5-17 years of age) with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis (confirmed by means of a positive grass pollen skin prick test response and serum-specific IgE measurement) received once-daily SLIT tablets or placebo. Cost-effectiveness of specific immunotherapy with Grazax in allergic rhinitis co-existing with asthma; Background: In the United Kingdom, approximately 10.8 million people suffer from asthma, placing an economic burden on the society of more than £2 billion per year. For allergic asthma, treatment options consist of allergen avoidance, symptomatic treatment and allergen-specific immunotherapy (SIT). Only SIT addresses the underlying cause of the disease, reducing symptoms and offering the potential for long-term improvement. Grazax - the first tablet-based SIT - is indicated for the treatment of patients with grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis, including those with co-existing asthma. Objective: To assess the cost-effectiveness of Grazax in patients with rhinoconjunctivitis and co-existing asthma. Methods: A prospective pharmacoeconomic analysis was carried out as part of a multinational clinical trial assessing the efficacy of Grazax (n = 79) compared with placebo (n = 72). Both groups had access to symptomatic medication
Sublingual immunotherapy: What have we learnt from the 'big trials'?; Purpose of review: Recent systematic reviews support the use of sublingual immunotherapy for allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in adults, whereas data in children have been less convincing. The present review evaluates three recent 'definitive' trials in adults and one in children. Recent findings: Two large independent randomized controlled trials of grass allergen tablets have confirmed the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy in adults with seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Effects were both allergen dose-dependent and time-dependent. Tablets were well tolerated and equally effective in monosensitized compared with polysensitized patients and in patients with peak seasonal asthma (patients with perennial asthma were specifically excluded). Local side effects were common but largely self-limiting and not bothersome. There were no serious treatment-related adverse events. Results were similar in magnitude to those observed in a comparable study of subcutaneous immunotherapy using an alum-based vaccine. A trial of sublingual drops in children with hayfever in a primary care setting was negative, although these results could not be generalized. Summary: Sublingual immunotherapy represents an effective and well tolerated treatment for seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in adults. Current ongoing paediatric trials and evaluation of long-term effects in adults will further define its role in therapy. © 2008 Wolters Kluwer Health | Lippincott Williams & Wilkins.

In order to investigate clinical and immunological changes induced by the administration of allergens via the oral mucosa, the double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized design of the trial with 30 other patients enrolled in placebo groups was applied. Specific immunotherapy with oral drops administered sublingually or supralingually was performed in the same way, keeping the drops under or on the tongue, respectively, for 1-2 min before swallowing them
Economic evaluation of sublingual vs subcutaneous allergen immunotherapy; Background: Sublingual allergen immunotherapy (SLIT) is a commonly used alternative route of administration to standard subcutaneous immunotherapy (SCIT) in Europe. Despite its wide use, the cost-effectiveness of SLIT vs SCIT has not been well established. Objective: To evaluate the cost and effectiveness of SLIT compared with SCIT in patients with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis during a 3-year specific allergen immunotherapy (SIT) from a third-party payer's, a patient's, and society's perspectives. Methods: We performed an open-label randomized clinical trial of patients receiving SLIT (n = 19), patients receiving SCIT (n = 23), and a control group (n = 22). The outcome measures were Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire score, visual analog scale score, symptomatic medication reduction, and direct and indirect costs. Results: SLIT offered clinical benefits to patients comparable to those provided by SCIT. From the perspective of a third-party payer, the total average direct medical cost per patient of 3-year SIT was estimated at €416 vs €482 in the SLIT and SCIT groups, respectively. A patient who received SLIT paid less than a patient who received SCIT for all out-of-pocket costs (€176 for SLIT vs €255 for SCIT) but more for sole allergen extracts (€72 for SLIT vs €55 for SCIT). When both direct and indirect costs were considered, the 3-year SIT expenditures per patient reached €684 vs €1,004 in the SLIT and SCIT groups, respectively. Conclusions: SLIT represents a less expensive alternative relative to subcutaneous administration from all perspectives. However, from a patient's perspective, SCIT offers a less expensive alternative for patients who do not experience loss of income and travel costs associated with treatment.
Grass pollen immunotherapy: IL-10 induction and suppression of late responses precedes IgG4 inhibitory antibody activity; Background: Grass pollen immunotherapy is an effective treatment for seasonal allergic rhinitis that provides the opportunity to study the induction and maintenance of allergen-specific immune tolerance. Objectives: We investigated the relationship between clinical responsiveness, regulatory cytokine production, and antibody responses to allergen during 1 year of immunotherapy. Methods: Eighteen subjects with severe seasonal allergic rhinitis were randomized double-blind to receive active or placebo injections of an alum-adsorbed grass pollen vaccine (Alutard SQ). Subjects underwent repeated testing of early- and late-phase skin responses to intradermal allergen, and cellular responses to grass pollen allergen were tested. Sera were tested for allergen-specific IgG4, IgA, and inhibitory activity in biologic assays of IgE responses. Results: Grass pollen immunotherapy was effective in reducing overall symptom scores (P < .05) and conjunctival reactivity (P < .05). In the active group significant IL-10 production occurred early at low allergen doses and at a similar time as inhibition of late skin responses at 2 to 4 weeks. Serum allergen-specific IgG4, IgA, and inhibitory antibody activity for basophil histamine release and IgE-facilitated allergen binding to B cells occurred later, at 6 to 12 weeks, at higher allergen doses and preceded inhibition of early skin responses. Conclusion: IL-10 responses occur early but at immunotherapy doses that are not clinically effective. Later induction of inhibitory antibodies, including IgG4 and IgA, might be required for efficacy through modulation of IgE-mediated events. © 2008 American Academy of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology.

Is specific immunotherapy now suitable for treating respiratory allergy?; Allergic rhinitis (AR) and asthma are inflammatory disorders of the nose and the bronchial airways induced by indoor and/or outdoor allergen exposure of the mucosa of the nose driving by IgE on-going production. Upper and lower respiratory allergic diseases can lead to a significant reduction in quality of life with a negative impact on work productivity, school performance and social activities, and represent a relevant direct and indirect health cost to society. The GINA and ARIA therapeutic approaches consider the use of different tools on the basis of the severity of the diseases with a stepwise approach. Many classes of drugs are currently available for the symptomatic treatment of AR and asthma. Allergen-specific immunotherapy (SIT) is a treatment designed to prevent allergic reaction from occurring, thus treating the basic cause of the disease process. SIT interferes with the basic mechanism of allergy and alters the natural course of allergic diseases, thus offering a long-lasting and preventive effect. Subcutaneous immunotherapy (SCIT) is the current standard of care. It is established as effective for patients with allergic rhinitis and/or asthma, especially for allergies to grass pollen, ragweed pollen, birch pollen, mountain cedar pollen, house dust mite, cladosporium, alternaria, and cats. Patients treated with SCIT are at increased risk of both local and systemic adverse reactions. In the majority of cases, symptoms were readily reversible with appropriate treatment. The problem of safety was a strong impulse to the search for alternative administration routes. In official documents, sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) is now considered as a viable alternative to the classic injection route for both adults and children with rhinitis and/or asthma.

Grazax--treatment for grass pollen hay fever; Grazax is approved in 27 European countries for treatment of grass pollen-induced hay fever in adults with a proper diagnosis and clinically-relevant symptoms. Grazax is put under the tongue where it dissolves instantly. Treatment should be initiated at least 8-16 weeks prior to the start of the grass pollen season, and similar to traditional immunotherapy the treatment should continue for three years. The recommended dose is one tablet daily. In this review the clinical documentation is briefly discussed. The demonstrated efficacy is clinically relevant and the safety profile is acceptable. Complete documentation of long-term effects is still pending.
Comparison of costs of sublingual immunotherapy and drug treatment in grass-pollen induced allergy: Results from the SIMAP database study; Objectives: This analysis is focused on the comparison of costs of allergic rhinitis (R) alone or with allergic asthma (R + A) in grass pollen allergy, for subjects treated with sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) and symptomatic drugs, versus standard care controls. Methods:The SIMAP (Sublingual IMmunotherapy in Allergic Patients) study is a longitudinal observational database operated by a network of Allergy centers. Patients suffering from grass pollen allergy were included in this analysis and assigned to SLIT (plus drugs as needed) or to treatment with drugs alone. Outcome measures included use of medications, SLIT, visits and tests. Costs were assessed from the perspective of the Italian National Health Service
Sublingual immunotherapy with standardized quality grass allergen tablets: Results of the development program; 15-25% of the German population suffer from allergic rhinoconjunctivitis, which is caused by grass pollen in half of the cases. Allergen-specific immunotherapy is considered the only causal treatment. For the first time a registered preparation for sublingual immunotherapy designed as a lyophilisated grass pollen tablet is available for the treatment of grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis. The tablet consists of standard quality- (SQ) standardized grass pollen allergens from timothy (Phleum pratense) in a dosage of 75.000 SQ-T. The application is once daily. This review summarizes the clinical studies for the evaluation of the safety and clinical efficacy of this new preparation. In the early phase of the development program, the main interest was related to safety issues. In total, 3 Phase I studies have been performed. In 1 of these studies patients were treated outside the pollen season with dosages of up to 1.000.000 SQ-T (> 13 times the dosage of the actual tablet) without severe or serious adverse events. In a dose-finding study, the optimal therapeutic dose was found to be 75.000 SQ-T once daily. With this dose the following studies were performed. In a Phase III study (GT-08) 634 patients were included. A mean reduction of the symptom score of 30% (p < 0.0001) and of the medication score of 38% (p < 0.0001) was demonstrated in the first treatment year. For the second treatment year (351 patients) a mean reduction of the symptom score of 36% (p < 0.0001) and of the medication score of 46% (p < 0.0001) was found. Serologic analyses revealed a modified systemic response of the immune system with a significant progessive increase of specific IgG4 antibodies after each treatment year. © 2008 Dustri-Verlag Dr. Karl Feistle.
Treatment of grass pollen allergy: Focus on a standardized grass allergen extract - Grazax®; Immunotherapy is the only treatment for allergy that has the potential to alter the natural course of the disease. Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) for grass pollen-induced rhino-conjunctivitis has been developed to make immunotherapy available to a broader group of allergic patients. In the largest clinical programme ever conducted with allergen-specific immunotherapy, over 1,700 adults and 260 children have been exposed to Grazax®. Grazax is formulated as an oral lyophilisate (tablet) for sublingual administration, containing 75,000 SQ-T standardized allergen extract of grass pollen from Phleum pratense. Grazax is indicated for treatment of grass pollen-induced rhinitis and conjunctivitis in adult patients with clinically relevant symptoms and diagnosed with a positive skin prick test and/or specific IgE test to grass pollen. In phase I trials doses from 2,500 to 1,000,000 SQ-T were tested. All doses were well tolerated and 75,000 SQ-T, with approximately 15 μg major allergen protein, was chosen as the optimal dose. Three phase III trials are ongoing, one being a long-term trial. Results from GT-08 trial first and second treatment years showed a reduction of 30% and 36%, respectively, in daily rhino-conjunctivitis symptom scores and a reduction of 38% and 46% of daily rhino-conjunctivitis medication scores compared with placebo over the entire grass pollen season. Subjects treated with Grazax also had an increased number of well days and improved quality of life, andmore subjects experienced excellent rhino-conjunctivitis control. The most common adverse events related to Grazax are local reactions, such as prinitus, edema mouth, ear pruritus, throat irritation, and sneezing. We conclude that Grazax is efficacious and safe for treatment of rhino-conjunctivitis due to grass pollen allergy. © 2008 Dove Medical Press Limited. All rights reserved.
Optimal dose, efficacy, and safety of once-daily sublingual immunotherapy with a 5-grass pollen tablet for seasonal allergic rhinitis; Background: Sublingual immunotherapy is well tolerated and data suggest its effectiveness for the treatment of allergic rhinitis in adults, but it lacks optimum dose definition. Objective: To assess the efficacy, safety, and optimal dose of grass pollen tablets for immunotherapy of patients with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: In this multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study, 628 adults with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis (confirmed by positive skin prick test and serum-specific IgE) received 1 of 3 doses of a standardized 5-grass pollen extract, or placebo, administered sublingually using a once-daily tablet formulation. The treatment was initiated 4 months before the estimated pollen season and continued throughout the season. The primary outcome was Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score
Sublingual immunotherapy in pollen-induced seasonal rhinitis and conjunctivitis: A randomized controlled trial; Background: Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) is a recognized and safe treatment for allergic rhinitis and conjunctivitis. The aim was to evaluate the efficacy and safety of tablets for grass and rye pollen-induced rhinitis and conjunctivitis. Methods: A double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial was carried out over 9 months. 105 patients received a standardized grass/rye mix extract or a placebo using sublingual drops during the build-up phase. Drops were replaced by sublingual tablets during the maintenance phase (300 IR/daily). Results: In patients that received active treatment, a significantly lower total symptom score (rhinitis and conjunctivitis) compared to the placebo group was observed (p = 0.038). The investigators' assessment revealed a significant improvement in favor of the active treatment group (p = 0.018). Skin reactivity to grass and rye pollen was significantly reduced in the active treatment group (p < 0.05). No statistical difference was observed between the two groups for serum-specific IgG4 levels. Side effects were local and mild, and no severe systemic reactions were reported. Conclusion: This study indicates that tablet-based sublingual immunotherapy was safe and significantly improved grass/rye pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms. It was also associated with a significant inhibition of the immediate skin response.
An extract of Timothy-grass pollen used as sublingual immunotherapy for summer hay fever; Grazax® is a lyophilisate of an extract of Timothy-grass pollen (Phleum pratense) administered by the sublingual route to induce desensitization (or hyposensitization) to grass pollen in subjects with hay fever. Since allergen avoidance measures are limited in hay fever sufferers, present treatment, at least in the United Kingdom, is almost always by symptomatic medication. The effectiveness of symptomatic treatment in hay fever is variable and depends on patient compliance and the judicious prescribing of antihistamines and anti-inflammatory preparations either alone or in combination. Desensitization (hyposensitization or specific immunotherapy) by subcutaneous injection has been shown to be very efficacious and is used for patients who do not adequately respond to drug treatment. A rare side effect of desensitizing injections is anaphylaxis, and so use is limited to specialized centers. For these reasons there has been considerable interest in specific immunotherapy by the sublingual route. Grazax® has recently been approved in the United Kingdom. It is commenced at least four months prior to the expected start of the grass pollen season and in line with injection immunotherapy treatment will be recommended for a period of three years with annual reviews to assess patient outcomes. Grazax® grass allergen tablets are well tolerated in patients with grass pollen allergy with most adverse events being mild local reactions. There have been no instances of anaphylaxis. In randomized double-blind placebo controlled trials Grazax® reduces symptoms and medication scores in adults with hay fever. The long-term effects of Grazax® are currently being investigated. © 2007 Prous Science. All rights reserved.

Grazax: an oral vaccine for the treatment of grass pollen allergy (hay fever).; Grazax is a self-administered, once-daily, tablet-based vaccine that offers an alternative to allergy shots for adults with grass pollen allergy (hay fever). (2) Evidence from three randomized controlled trials indicates modest improvements in hay fever symptoms, with reduced use of medication to control symptoms (rescue medication use) in adults who took Grazax compared with placebo. No studies have compared Grazax with injection-based allergen immunotherapy. (3) It is not yet known if patients treated with Grazax will have a sustained tolerance to grass pollen following treatment discontinuation. (4) Adverse effects of Grazax are generally mild to moderate local allergic reactions of short duration, and include itching and swelling of the mouth, and throat irritation. (5) If Grazax becomes widely prescribed and is covered by provincial drug plans, the costs to the Canadian health care system and the impact on allergy specialist services could be substantial.
Cost-effectiveness of GRAZAX® for prevention of grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis in Southern Europe; Background: Allergic rhinoconjunctivitis is a global health problem. Around 14 million people in Spain, France, Italy, and Austria suffer from grass pollen induced allergic rhinitis. Standard care only provides symptoms relief, while allergen specific immunotherapy (SIT) treats the underlying cause of the disease. Grazax® from ALK-Abelló is a new, tablet-based, effective route of SIT for home treatment. The objective was to assess the cost-effectiveness of Grazax® in four Southern European countries. Methods: A prospective pharmacoeconomic analyses was carried out alongside a multinational, clinical trial measuring the efficacy of Grazax®. Pooled data on resource use and health outcomes were collected. A societal perspective was adopted, and the analysis had a nine-year time horizon. The primary outcome measure was quality adjusted life years (QALYs). Results: Grazax® was superior to standard care for all efficacy endpoints, including QALYs gained, and resulted in significantly less use of rescue medication and fewer hours missed from work. Grazax® was cost-effective for all countries for an annual price in the range of €1500-€1900. The result was improved by inclusion of future costs of asthma and exclusion of Spanish trial centers which experienced an exceptionally low pollen season. Conclusion: The analysis illustrates that allergen SIT with Grazax® for grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis is a cost-effective intervention in Southern Europe. © 2007.
Safety of specific sublingual immunotherapy with SQ standardized grass allergen tablets in children; The aim of the study was to confirm the safety of an orodispersible grass allergen tablet 75,000 SQ-T (Grazax®, ALK-Abelló A/S, Hørsholm, Denmark) in children aged 5-12 yr. The study was randomized, double-blinded and placebo-controlled. Sixty children aged 5-12 yr suffering from grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis (with or without asthma) from five centres in two countries (three in Germany and two in Spain) participated in the study. They were randomized at the ratio of 3:1 as receiving either Grazax or placebo tablet given sublingually once daily for 28 days outside the grass pollen season. A total of 810 treatment-related adverse events were reported in the Grazax group. The majority of these were local reactions in the mouth or throat and were mostly mild (71%) to moderate (27%) in severity and resolved within days. Thirty-five (78%) subjects treated with Grazax and five (33%) treated with placebo reported at least one treatment-related adverse event. Oral pruritus, throat irritation, mouth oedema and ear pruritus appeared as the most frequently reported treatment-related adverse events. 62% (28 of 45) of the actively treated subjects reported oral pruritus, 36% (16 of 45) throat irritation, 31% (14 of 45) mouth oedema and 22% (10 of 45) ear pruritus. Two actively treated subjects withdrew from the study: one subject due to four adverse events (moderate eye pruritus, moderate pharyngolaryngeal pain, moderate non-cardiac chest pain and moderate dysphagia) and one subject due to a serious adverse event (asthmatic attack). The subjects recovered completely from the events. In conclusion, in the present study, Grazax was in general tolerated in a paediatric population and considered suitable for further clinical investigations in children. © 2007 The Authors.
